</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Using the one-strain-many-compounds (OSMAC) culturing approach, metabolomic studies, and bioassay-guided purification, we have isolated and characterised three new chlorinated natural products, agelolines B-D (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>3</strong>), together with two known compounds, ageloline A (<strong>4</strong>) and gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>), which have been identified by high-resolution mass spectrometry and 1D and 2D NMR analyses. The preliminary evaluation of three small-scale extracts (M400, R358 and SGG) against the fish pathogen, <em>Aeromonas salmonicida</em> subsp. <em>achromogenes</em> KELDUR265-87, showed that the R358 extract displayed significant activity. Furthermore, the natural products (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong>) were evaluated against the fish pathogen <em>Aeromonas salmonicida</em> and human pathogens (<em>Stenotrophomonas maltophilia</em> L2125, <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> ATCC6538P, and <em>S. pneumoniae</em> L44) using a serial dilution assay. Compound <strong>3</strong> displayed activity against <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> ATCC6538P, <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125, and <em>S. pneumoniae</em> L44 with MIC values of 6, 32, and 64 µg/mL, respectively. Interestingly, only gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>) exhibited considerable inhibition against <em>A. salmonicida</em> with an MIC value of 32 µg/mL, and the activity increased by two-fold when supplemented with 0.45 mM calcium salt, while <strong>2</strong> and <strong>4</strong> showed moderate inhibition against <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125. The biosynthetic pathways of compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>4</strong> were proposed. This is the first report of specific inhibition of <em>A. salmonicida</em> by <strong>5</strong>.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Streptomyces</em> spp., antimicrobial activity, halogenated compound, <em>A. salmonicida</em>, lipopeptides, feature-based molecular networking, metaboanalyst</p></section></section><section id="sec1-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Aquaculture is one of the fastest growing food production systems within the agrifood sector, with a 54% increase in production over the period 2010–2019, reaching levels of 94 million tonnes of seafood in 2022 [<a href="#B1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-marinedrugs-23-00362">1</a>], and it continues to be an important source of food for the world’s population [<a href="#B2-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-marinedrugs-23-00362">2</a>]. The global aquaculture market was valued at USD 311.1 billion in 2023 and is projected to increase to USD 573.7 billion by 2035 at a 5.2% compound annual growth rate [<a href="#B3-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-marinedrugs-23-00362">3</a>]. With the ongoing increase in food consumption globally, aquaculture will continue to play an important role as a food source and in doing so help with global food security, while addressing nutritional deficiencies and malnutrition challenges against the backdrop of an escalating world population [<a href="#B2-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-marinedrugs-23-00362">2</a>]. One of the main challenges facing the aquaculture industry, particularly as it intensifies, is controlling a number of diseases caused by pathogenic bacteria, which can result in significant economic damage due to morbidity and death, which in 2014 was estimated to be in the region of USD 6 billion annually [<a href="#B4-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-marinedrugs-23-00362">4</a>].</p>
<p>In this context, <em>Aeromonas salmonicida</em> (Family Aeromonadaceae, Phylum Pseudomonadota) is an important fish pathogen that constitutes the only known non-motile species in its genus, which comprises five subspecies which cause typical (subsp. <em>salmonicida</em>) or atypical (subsp. <em>achromogenes</em>, subsp. <em>masoucida</em>, subsp. <em>pectinolytica</em>, and subsp. <em>smithia</em>) furunculosis, soft tissue rot, fin rot, and haemorrhagic septicaemia in salmonid fish [<a href="#B5-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-marinedrugs-23-00362">5</a>].</p>
<p>Antibiotics are commonly used in the aquaculture sector to treat fish during disease outbreaks, but their efficacy is often undermined due to the target bacteria developing antibiotic resistance resulting from the frequent use of these antibiotics and their subsequent persistence in the aquatic environment [<a href="#B6-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-marinedrugs-23-00362">6</a>]. For example, the plasmidome of <em>A. salmonicida</em> is known to contain plasmids conferring resistance against chloramphenicol, spectinomycin, streptomycin, sulfonamides, tetracycline, mercury, and quaternary ammonium compounds [<a href="#B7-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-marinedrugs-23-00362">7</a>,<a href="#B8-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-marinedrugs-23-00362">8</a>]. A recent genomic-based study of <em>A. salmonicida</em> GMT3 further highlights the antibiotic resistance characteristics of the strain with genes potentially involved in resistance to quinolones, ampicillin, streptogramin, chloramphenicol, and polymyxin, together with genes encoding efflux pump systems [<a href="#B9-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-marinedrugs-23-00362">9</a>]. <em>Aeromonas</em> spp. are also known to be able to transfer antibiotic resistance genes in different environmental ecosystems, and to survive as biofilms on biotic or abiotic surfaces, thereby facilitating the exchange in antimicrobial resistance genes within these ecosystems [<a href="#B10-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-marinedrugs-23-00362">10</a>].</p>
<p>Thus, a more sustainable control strategy to protect fish from these disease outbreaks is required. In this respect the introduction or augmentation of beneficial microbes and their use in aquaculture as probiotics is considered an extremely promising approach to reduce fish diseases [<a href="#B11-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-marinedrugs-23-00362">11</a>,<a href="#B12-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-marinedrugs-23-00362">12</a>,<a href="#B13-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-marinedrugs-23-00362">13</a>]. A stable and resilient microbiome is intimately involved in helping maintain overall good fish health by assisting the fish host in its defence against colonization and infection by pathogens. Specific microbes, or microbial consortia, and their metabolites are known to protect fish against these pathogens by enhancing stress resistance and immunity [<a href="#B12-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-marinedrugs-23-00362">12</a>,<a href="#B14-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-marinedrugs-23-00362">14</a>,<a href="#B15-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-marinedrugs-23-00362">15</a>]. In particular a number of actinobacterial-based probiotics have been successfully employed in aquaculture systems, including <em>Streptomyces</em> strains [<a href="#B16-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-marinedrugs-23-00362">16</a>], with for example <em>S. panacagri</em> and <em>S. floculus</em> being reported to display antagonistic activity against <em>Vibrio harveyi</em>, <em>V. parahaemolyticus</em>, and <em>V. vulnificus</em> [<a href="#B17-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-marinedrugs-23-00362">17</a>].</p>
<p>Recent years have seen technological advances in areas such as high-throughput screening, genomics, and associated bioinformatics, as well as metabolomics and related chemoinformatics, which have accelerated the discovery, dereplication, and further characterisation of marine natural products [<a href="#B18-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-marinedrugs-23-00362">18</a>,<a href="#B19-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-marinedrugs-23-00362">19</a>]. The integration of these technologies, such as with the Paired Omics Data Platform [<a href="#B20-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-marinedrugs-23-00362">20</a>] and with such tools as FERMO [<a href="#B21-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-marinedrugs-23-00362">21</a>] and nanoRAPIDS [<a href="#B22-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-marinedrugs-23-00362">22</a>], has streamlined the integration of complementary workflows, making detailed characterisations of the biosynthetic routes and structural characteristics of the chemical entities responsible for observed bioactivities much easier. The integration of high-throughput metabolomics and genomic sequencing has allowed for the targeted prioritisation of novel metabolites while linking this novel chemistry to their biosynthetic gene clusters from large libraries of bacteria [<a href="#B23-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-marinedrugs-23-00362">23</a>,<a href="#B24-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-marinedrugs-23-00362">24</a>] and fungi [<a href="#B25-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-marinedrugs-23-00362">25</a>]. These studies have enabled the discovery of the anti-cancer peptide tambromycin, produced by <em>Streptomyces</em> spp. from a collection of actinomycetes, and also the discovery of 20 members of a new class of non-ribosomal peptides produced by <em>Micromonospora</em> spp. [<a href="#B24-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-marinedrugs-23-00362">24</a>].</p>
<p>In an effort to identify a marine-derived antibiotic that may be useful in preventing the growth of various fish pathogens, we isolated <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 from the deep-sea sponge <em>Lissodendoryx diversichela</em> [<a href="#B26-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-marinedrugs-23-00362">26</a>]. In the fermentation process of the isolated bacterial strain, an OSMAC (one-strain-many-compounds) strategy was adopted to enhance production of unique secondary metabolites with the aim of expressing cryptic genes by altering the media composition of the culture broth [<a href="#B27-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-marinedrugs-23-00362">27</a>,<a href="#B28-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-marinedrugs-23-00362">28</a>,<a href="#B29-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-marinedrugs-23-00362">29</a>,<a href="#B30-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-marinedrugs-23-00362">30</a>]. The carbon and nitrogen sources, including the salt content of the growth media composition, were considered in particular in the selection of the three media (R358, M400, and SGG) in the fermentation process. We report here on the use of metabolomic studies and bioassay-guided isolation in the identification of a unique and bioactive extract that led to the discovery of three new chlorinated natural products and the first report of a gausemycin-type compound with growth inhibition activity against the gram-negative fish pathogen, <em>A. salmonicida</em>.</p></section><section id="sec2-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results and Discussion</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Non-Targeted Multivariate Analysis of OSMAC Extracts</h3>
<p><em>Streptomyces</em> strain B188M101 was fermented in different growth media (SGG, M400, and R358) and in three biological replicates together with their respective process blanks. An untargeted metabolomic study was employed to determine the best culture media suitable for the growth and elicitation of uncommon bioactive secondary metabolites from the actinobacterial strain [<a href="#B31-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-marinedrugs-23-00362">31</a>,<a href="#B32-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-marinedrugs-23-00362">32</a>]. Hence, the extracts obtained were subjected to comparative metabolomic studies to uncover extracts with unique chemical diversity and specificity. Understanding the chemical space in an LC-MS dataset of a microorganism fermented under different conditions requires specialized unsupervised learning techniques: principal component analysis (PCA) [<a href="#B33-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-marinedrugs-23-00362">33</a>,<a href="#B34-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-marinedrugs-23-00362">34</a>], one-factor statistical analysis, including ANOVA, and the use of a Hierarchical Clustering Dendrogram [<a href="#B33-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-marinedrugs-23-00362">33</a>,<a href="#B35-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-marinedrugs-23-00362">35</a>].</p>
<p>The centroided raw data of positive mode ionisation of each extract generated from high-resolution Orbitrap IQ-X mass spectroscopy (Thermo Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA) was converted to the mzML format and further pre-processed on the MZmine software version 4.2.0 to produce feature peak lists and intensity data required for metaboAnalyst software version 6.0. Precursor mass ions from the UPLC solvent and media blanks were removed from the sample data before downstream analysis to prevent distortion of the results.</p>
<p>Of the 1508 molecular ion peaks obtained with an average of 167.8 peaks per sample, a total of 63.45% missing values were detected, resulting in a wide variability of metabolites amongst the extract datasets to be compared; this is a common occurrence in untargeted LC-MS data, in this case, due to the chemical diversity.</p>
<p>The principal component analysis (PCA) plot is displayed in <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>A clearly shows the relationship between the three extracts based on chemical profile similarity [<a href="#B36-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-marinedrugs-23-00362">36</a>]. The first principal component (PC1) that covers the largest variance in the dataset followed by the second principal component (PC2), orthogonal to PC1 with 61.3% and 13.3%, confirmed the extent of the shared secondary metabolite distribution amongst the three extracts. However, the R358 extract was an outlier compared to the SGG and M400 extracts, being located at a distant quadrant from the respective two clusters.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f001"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/e80046aad710/marinedrugs-23-00362-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="422" width="770" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(<strong>A</strong>) Principal Component Analysis (PCA) score plot. Pink circles correspond to the M400 extract; green circles correspond to the R358 extract; navy blue circles correspond to the SGG extract. (<strong>B</strong>) Hierarchical Clustering Dendrogram (samples were clustered using the Ward method and Euclidean distance metric): the same colour representation as (<strong>A</strong>) using squares.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The results show a low <em>p</em>-value of 0.0045 in comparison with the software default setting <em>p</em>-value of 0.05, indicating strong evidence that the variation is statistically significant. The extract datasets were further analysed using a Hierarchical Clustering Dendrogram, which as previously mentioned is another unsupervised technique used to perform exploratory data analysis (EDA) that allows for in-depth understanding of skewed data by identifying the main features which can be visualised in dendrogram formation, that is, a binary merge tree [<a href="#B37-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-marinedrugs-23-00362">37</a>,<a href="#B38-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-marinedrugs-23-00362">38</a>,<a href="#B39-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-marinedrugs-23-00362">39</a>]. The R358 metabolites appear as a distinct cluster from the other two extract metabolites, supporting the variation observed in the two-dimensional space established by the PCA score plot (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>A). Thus, it appears that the <em>Streptomyces</em> B188M101 produced shared groups of metabolites when fermented in SGG and M400 media.</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Preliminary Disc Diffusion Assays</h3>
<p>The M400, R358, and SGG extracts were screened against <em>Aeromonas salmonicida</em>, using a disc diffusion assay following a previously described method [<a href="#B40-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-marinedrugs-23-00362">40</a>]. Following 24 h of incubation, significant inhibition was observed from the R358 extract at a concentration of 8 mg/mL with a zone of inhibition of 20 mm which was comparable to the 30 mm zone of inhibition observed from the broad-spectrum antibiotic gentamicin tested at 10 µg (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A). No activity was observed from the M400 or SGG extracts (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A) or from the media control discs (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/d613702a5632/marinedrugs-23-00362-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="326" width="739" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(<strong>A</strong>) Disc diffusion assay showing zone of inhibition of the M400, R358, and SGG extracts and gentamicin antibiotic (P) against <em>A. salmonicida</em>. (<strong>B</strong>) Media control of M400, R358, SGG, and dried methanol discs as negative control (N).</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot3-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Chemoinformatic Feature-Based Molecular Networking and Database Dereplication Analysis</h3>
<p>Both the one-factor-statistical analysis and disc diffusion assay with <em>A. salmonicida</em> established that the R358 extract contained bioactive secondary metabolites. Thus, we subsequently subjected the LC-MS/MS ESI-Orbitrap data of the three extracts to feature-based molecular networking (FBMN) [<a href="#B41-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-marinedrugs-23-00362">41</a>] analysis. FBMN is a computational method that bridges mass spectrometry data processing tools for LC-MS/MS and molecular networking analysis on the Global Natural Products Social Networking (GNPS) site and was employed here to investigate the molecular ecosystem and annotate new compounds in the crude extracts SGG, R358, and M400 by visualising the entire integrated features of the metabolomes. This type of bioinformatic technique mainly utilises the mass fragmentation information of the node (mass peak) in each dataset to determine their spectrum and feature similarity, hence, organising the family of molecules in clusters. The raw files of the Orbitrap MS were converted to mzML format using MSConvert software [<a href="#B42-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-marinedrugs-23-00362">42</a>], followed by pre-processing with MZmine 4.2.0 version to create a quantitative MS1 file and MGF files of fragment ions [<a href="#B43-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-marinedrugs-23-00362">43</a>]. The results of the analysis are publicly available at <a href="https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/result.jsp?task=a5069300445a407d90c4b80162125c2c&amp;view=download_cytoscape_data" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://gnps.ucsd.edu/ProteoSAFe/result.jsp?task=a5069300445a407d90c4b80162125c2c&amp;view=download_cytoscape_data</a>, accessed on 23 May 2025. Analysis of the GNPS data on Cytoscape 3.10.0 [<a href="#B44-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-marinedrugs-23-00362">44</a>,<a href="#B45-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-marinedrugs-23-00362">45</a>] showed 596 molecular features arranged in clusters and singletons after removal of features (1285 nodes) belonging to SGG, R358, and M400 media controls (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>A).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/a8e70e602839/marinedrugs-23-00362-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="999" width="794" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Global molecular network of the marine-derived <em>Streptomyces</em> B188M101 extracts (M400, R358 and SGG). (<strong>A</strong>) Overall molecular network of M400, R358, SGG, and media control extracts; red circles highlight three unique chlorinated clusters. (<strong>B</strong>) Clearer view of molecular mass clusters of putative new chlorinated compounds including the known gausemycin antibiotics.</p></figcaption></figure><p>It is worth noting that 15 of the 31 molecular clusters that contained two or more features in the molecular network were specific to the R358 extract. The remaining clusters consisted of features found in either M400 or SGG extracts, further corroborating the chemical variation observed using the multivariance one-factor analysis (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). The natural product database search on the GNPS website (GNPS; <a href="http://gnps.ucsd.edu" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://gnps.ucsd.edu</a>; accessed on 14 June 2025) of the identified features detected in the molecular network clusters (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>A) did not show any potential match. Hence, manual dereplication processing using the Thermo Scientific FreeStyle™ 1.8 SP2 QF1 (Thermo Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA) was utilised to analyse the MS spectrum of the R358 extract and chemically profile the precursor ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> for each base peak organised according to their retention time. Also, molecular formulae in accordance with the mass isotopic pattern matching [<a href="#B46-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-marinedrugs-23-00362">46</a>], theoretical mass, and mass error within 3 ppm mass accuracy were established [<a href="#B47-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-marinedrugs-23-00362">47</a>,<a href="#B48-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-marinedrugs-23-00362">48</a>]. The dereplication of the distinctive clusters gave 23 new molecular features whose elemental compositions were consistent with chlorinated isotopic signatures and intense doubly charged base peaks (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S1</a> and <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>B). In addition, 24 putative new non-halogenated molecules (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>) and 4 known gausemycins A–D derivatives [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>] and ageloline A [<a href="#B51-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-marinedrugs-23-00362">51</a>] (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S1</a> and <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>) were annotated following an assessment of both the accurate exact masses and the corresponding molecular formulae on the natural product databases in combination with MS/MS fragmentation analysis.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/74b2c7eb77d5/marinedrugs-23-00362-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="466" width="770" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>The known compounds identified in the R358 extract of <em>Streptomyces</em> B188M101.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Earlier work on these natural lipoglycopeptides led to the isolation of gausemycin A and B from the fermentation broth of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>]. Other minor analogues, C–F, were subsequently isolated and structurally elucidated from the same bacterial strain [<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>]. According to the high-resolution Orbitrap IQ-X ESI-MS, doubly charged intense precursor ion peaks at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 923.9016, 959.4201, 966.4280, and 857.8801(M+2H)<sup>2+</sup> corresponding to molecular formulae of C<sub>84</sub>H<sub>118</sub>ClO<sub>28</sub>N<sub>17</sub>, C<sub>87</sub>H<sub>123</sub>ClO<sub>29</sub>N<sub>18</sub>, C<sub>88</sub>H<sub>125</sub>ClO<sub>29</sub>N<sub>18</sub>, and C<sub>79</sub>H<sub>110</sub>ClO<sub>24</sub>N<sub>17</sub>, were attributed, respectively, to gausemycins A–D [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>]. Their structural scaffold consisted of a cyclic core and a tail which were made up of amino acids sequences and non-proteogenic amino acids, 2-amino-4-hydroxy-4-phenylbutyric acid (Ahpb), 2,3-diaminobutyric acid (Dab), chlorinated L-4-chlorokynurenine (ClKyn), and sugar moieties (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>).</p>
<p>A detailed MS<sup>2</sup> analysis of gausemycin A (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 1846.7936, [M+H]<sup>+</sup>) was performed using high-energy collisional dissociation (HCD), selected due to the presence of a cyclic core that is less amenable to fragmentation via low-energy collision-induced dissociation (CID). The resulting fragmentation spectra revealed characteristic b- and y-ions corresponding to the N- and C-terminal regions, respectively. Fragment ions of both lower and higher <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> values were observed, representing sequences from the linear tail region as well as the cyclic core structure of gausemycin A. A detailed explanation as to the MS/MS fragmentation of gausemycin A is provided in the <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">supplementary information (Figures S1 and S2)</a>.</p>
<p>Gausemycin B and C whose molecular ions were deduced as <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 1917.8325 and 1931.8458, with respective intense doubly charge ions at 966.42587 and 959.4201[M+2H]<sup>+2</sup>, differ from gausemycin A by 71.0487 and 87.0487 Da, confirming the addition of β-alanine and methyl-β-alanine as previously reported [<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>] and corroborated by the MS/MS fragmentation analysis (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figures S3 and S4</a>). The absence of sugar moieties in the MS/MS fragments of gausemycin D (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 1714.7521 [M+H]<sup>+1</sup>) showed it was consistent with the reported data [<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>]. The variation amongst known compound structures of gausemycins has been established to exist in the fatty acid chain and the attachment of amino acid building blocks on the amine functionality of ornithine at the tail region [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>]. For instance, the molecular ions observed at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 864.8885 were dereplicated as putative new congeners of gausemycin D, corresponding to the deduced molecular formula C<sub>80</sub>H<sub>112</sub>ClO<sub>24</sub>N<sub>17</sub> (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S1 and Figure S5</a>). The mass difference of 14.0176 Da relative to gausemycin D, supported by MS/MS experiments, suggests the direct incorporation of monomethyl on the ornithine side-chain amine group (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S6</a>). This interpretation is further corroborated by the clustering of molecular ions in the molecular networking analysis (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Additionally, fragment ion analysis of doubly charged molecular ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 930.9088 and 973.4351, corresponding to molecular formulas C<sub>85</sub>H<sub>120</sub>ClO<sub>28</sub> N<sub>17</sub> and C<sub>89</sub>H<sub>127</sub>ClO<sub>29</sub>N<sub>18</sub> (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S1, and Figures S7 and S9</a>), revealed structural features consistent with gausemycin A. However, these respective analogues exhibited additional modifications, specifically monomethyl and dimethyl-β-alanine substituents on the ornithine amine group (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figures S8 and S10</a>). Recent findings provide mechanistic insights into the biosynthetic pathway by which non-standard residues, such as ornithine, are methylated through methyltransferase domains embedded within non-ribosomal peptide synthetase (NRPS) systems [<a href="#B52-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-marinedrugs-23-00362">52</a>,<a href="#B53-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-marinedrugs-23-00362">53</a>,<a href="#B54-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-marinedrugs-23-00362">54</a>]. While this dereplication process has revealed the presence of several previously uncharacterised molecules within the R358 extract, the present study is now directed towards their targeted isolation and comprehensive structural characterisation.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Isolation and Structure Elucidation of New Chlorinated Compounds</h3>
<p>A large-scale culture (8L) of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was grown on R358 medium in a closed system shaker at 28 °C for 10 days, yielding 16.45 g of combined extract. The extract was fractionated following a modified Kupchan solvent partitioning method [<a href="#B55-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-marinedrugs-23-00362">55</a>], and upon dereplication analysis, subsequent purification of the butanol fraction resulted in the isolation of three new chlorinated compounds, designated as agelolines B–D (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>3</strong>), and two known compounds, ageloline A (<strong>4</strong>) [<a href="#B51-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-marinedrugs-23-00362">51</a>] and gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>) [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>] (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>), as outlined in the materials and methods section.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/484fe3efb0c6/marinedrugs-23-00362-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="694" width="747" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Structures of agelolines B–D (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>3</strong>), ageloline A (<strong>4</strong>), and gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>).</p></figcaption></figure><p>Compound <strong>1</strong> was isolated as yellowish powder. The high-resolution electron spray ionisation Orbitrap mass spectroscopy of <strong>1</strong> revealed an isotopic pattern indicating the presence of a chlorinated atom at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 285.0638/287.0607 (M+H, Δ + 0.5 ppm), and assigned the molecular formula C<sub>12</sub>H<sub>14</sub>ClN<sub>2</sub>O<sub>4</sub>, requiring 7 degrees of unsaturation (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S11</a>). The 1D and 2D NMR data shows the presence of signals consistent with three sp<sup>2</sup> aromatic methines, one sp<sup>3</sup> aliphatic methine, one sp<sup>3</sup> methylene, one sp<sup>3</sup> methyl, and six sp<sup>2</sup> quaternary carbons (see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figures S12–S14</a>). Detailed analysis of <sup>1</sup>H NMR chemical shifts, coupling constants, and COSY data suggested the presence of a 1,2,4-trisubstituted aromatic ring exhibiting coupling from H-5 (δ<sub>H</sub> 6.56, <em>J</em> = 8.7, 2.2) to both H-6 (δ<sub>H</sub> 7.72, <em>J</em> = 8.7) and H-3 (δ<sub>H</sub> 6.77, <em>J</em> = 2.2). The assignment of aromatic substructure, including the quaternary carbons to positions C-1 (δ<sub>C</sub> 116.7), C-2 (δ<sub>C</sub> 153.3) and C-4 (δ<sub>C</sub> 141.0), were based on the chemical shifts and strong HMBC correlation from H-5 to C-1 and C-3 (δ<sub>C</sub> 116.9), from H-6 to C-2 and C-4, and from H-3 to C-1 and C-5 (<em>δ<sub>C</sub></em> 115.9) (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S15 and Table S2</a>). The downfield chemical shifts at δ<sub>C</sub> 153.3 and 141.0 were due to the presence of primary amine and chlorine substituents at C-2 and C-4, respectively.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/marinedrugs-23-00362-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/5ecb0fdbad08/marinedrugs-23-00362-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="304" width="754" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Key HMBC, <sup>1</sup>H—<sup>1</sup>H COSY and NOESY correlations of compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>3</strong>.</p></figcaption></figure><p>The aliphatic substructure of <strong>1</strong> was constructed using the COSY spectrum, showing a spin system evident by the coupling between H-9 (δ<sub>H</sub> 4.89, m) and the diastereotopic protons H<sub>2</sub>-8 (δ<sub>H</sub> 3.54, <em>J</em> = 17.3, 6.3); 3.45, <em>J</em> = 16.9, 6.3) and a cross-peak HMBC correlation from H<sub>2</sub>-8 to C-6 (δ<sub>C</sub> 133.6), C-7 (δ<sub>C</sub> 198.1), C-9 (δ<sub>C</sub> 49.3), and C-10 (δ<sub>C</sub> 173.1, carboxylic acid unit), and from H-9 to C-7, C-8 (δ<sub>C</sub> 41.2), C-10, and to the acetyl carbonyl carbon C-11 (δ<sub>C</sub> 172.9). When the NMR data for compound <strong>1</strong> was recorded in deuterated DMSO (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S16</a>), <sup>1</sup>H-<sup>1</sup>H coupling was observed from H-9 to the amide proton (δ<sub>H</sub> 8.05, NH), which supported the acetyl group (C-11; δ<sub>C</sub> 21.8, C-12); this was corroborated by a NOESY correlation from the amide proton to methyl H<sub>3</sub>-12 (δ<sub>H</sub> 1.97) (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S17</a>) and HMBC correlation of singlet methyl protons H<sub>3</sub>-12 to C-11 (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). Hence, the complete structure of compound <strong>1</strong>, designated as ageloline B, confirmed it to be structurally related to the previously characterised antichlamydial quinolone ageloline A isolated from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. SBT345 [<a href="#B51-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-marinedrugs-23-00362">51</a>].</p>
<p>Compound <strong>2</strong> was isolated as a light yellowish powder, and its molecular formula was obtained from the ESI Orbitrap MS with a characteristic chlorinated isotopic cluster at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 299.0795/301.0764 (M+H, Δ +0.5 ppm) with 7 degrees of unsaturation (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S18</a>). The complete 1D and 2D NMR data (see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S3 and Figures S19–S22</a>) confirmed <strong>2</strong> to be similar to <strong>1</strong> except for the presence in its spectra of signals corresponding to a methoxy group. A cross-peak HMBC correlation from the methyl proton signal (δ<sub>H</sub> 3.72, H<sub>3</sub>-13) to carbonyl carbon C-10 supported the attachment (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). To assess whether compound <strong>2</strong> was an artefact resulting from spontaneous methylation induced by methanol during the extraction process—despite its detection in the LC-MS profile of the crude extract—a 50 mL culture was prepared and extracted using <em>sec</em>-butanol instead of methanol. The resulting extract was analysed via ESI-Orbitrap MS. The LC-MS chromatogram confirmed the presence of both compounds <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong>, with base peaks observed at retention times of 9.72 and 10.59 min, respectively (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S23</a>). Notably, methanol was excluded from all subsequent extraction, fractionation, and purification steps.</p>
<p>The absolute configurations of compounds <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong> were logically assigned as <strong>L</strong> based on extensive prior work demonstrating that 4-chloro-L-kynurenine (L-4-ClKyn)—a core substructure of both compounds—is an integral component of natural peptide antibiotics [<a href="#B56-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-marinedrugs-23-00362">56</a>]. This non-proteinogenic, modified amino acid has been identified as part of the building block sequence in lipopeptide antibiotics such as taromycin-type compounds and lipoglycopeptide antibiotics [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>,<a href="#B56-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-marinedrugs-23-00362">56</a>], and is also reported in the present study. Further support for the L-configuration was provided by optical rotation measurements, which yielded a negative value, consistent with the expected behaviour of L-4-ClKyn derivatives and other L-amino acids. Based on this conclusion, structure <strong>2</strong> was determined and named ageloline C.</p>
<p>Ageloline D (<strong>3</strong>) was isolated as a reddish yellow oil, with a molecular formula of C<sub>10</sub>H<sub>7</sub>ClO<sub>3</sub>N deduced from the molecular ion in the positive mode ESI Orbitrap MS at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 172.0163 (M+H, Δ + 1.3 ppm) corresponding to 5 degrees of unsaturation (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S24</a>). The <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectrum of <strong>3</strong> showed coupling patterns suggestive of the same 1,2,4-trisubstituted aromatic ring found in <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong>. The carboxyl functionality was placed at C-7 (δ<sub>C</sub> 170.8) due to the downfield chemical shift and a strong HMBC cross peak from H-6. The 1D and 2D NMR spectra data (see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S4 and Figures S25–S28</a>), together with the mass spectrometry analysis, established <strong>3</strong> as 2-amino-4-chlorobenzoic acid—a previously unreported natural product and a structural motif shared by <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong>.</p>
<p>Compound <strong>4</strong> was obtained as a yellowish powder which displayed a protonated ion, [M+H]<sup>+</sup>, in the positive mode HRESIMS at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 224.0109, from which the molecular formula of <strong>4</strong> was deduced as C<sub>10</sub>H<sub>7</sub>ClO<sub>3</sub>N (Δ: 0 ppm, 8 degrees of unsaturation, calcd. for C<sub>10</sub>H<sub>7</sub>ClO<sub>3</sub>N, 224.0109) (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S29</a>). The comparison of 1D, 2D NMR data as well as the MS data of 4 (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S5, Figures S30–S33</a>) with previously reported experimental data for ageloline A confirmed its identity [<a href="#B51-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-marinedrugs-23-00362">51</a>].</p>
<p>Compound <strong>5</strong> was isolated as a white solid and peaks in its Orbitrap HR-ESI-MS spectrum at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 1846.7936 with a characteristic chlorinated pattern and a doubly charged ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 923.9016, both in agreement with a molecular formula of C<sub>84</sub>H<sub>118</sub>ClO<sub>28</sub> N<sub>17</sub> and 34 degrees of unsaturation (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S34</a>). The structure of <strong>5</strong> was determined from full NMR spectroscopy data measured in DMSO-d6 at 318 K, and the MS/MS fragmentation pattern as suggested in the chemical dereplication section (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Tables S6 and S7 and Figures S1, S2 and S35–S37</a>). The absolute configurations of all the proteinogenic amino acids in <strong>5</strong> were established by the Marfey experiment, which was performed following chromatographic analysis by reverse-phase analytical HPLC with a wavelength of 340 nm of the hydrolysate and the standard derivatised amino acids (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S8</a>). The structure of <strong>5</strong> was confirmed as gausemycin A after a thorough check with the literature data [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot5-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Genomics of B188M101</h3>
<p><em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was taxonomically classified as a member of the <em>S. fimicarius</em> subgroup (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S38</a>). The identification and characterisation of the chlorinated compounds (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong>) allowed us to explore the B188M101 genome and identify a putative halogenase gene, namely a tryptophan-7-halogenase encoding gene within a 111.8 Kb biosynthetic gene cluster (BGC). The BGC was annotated by antiSMASH version 7.0 as a NRPS/betalactone hybrid cluster comprising 56 ORFs (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S9</a>). The BGCs most closely related to the NRPS/betalactone cluster from <em>Streptomyes</em> sp. B188M101 are those which produce compound <strong>5</strong> (gausemycin A) and gausemycin B from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>], the cadasides- [<a href="#B57-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-marinedrugs-23-00362">57</a>], and malacidins- [<a href="#B58-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-marinedrugs-23-00362">58</a>] producing clusters from soil metagenome cosmid libraries, and the friulimicins-producing cluster from <em>Actinoplanes friuliensis</em> [<a href="#B59-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-marinedrugs-23-00362">59</a>] (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S39</a>). The products of these clusters are cyclic lipopeptides or lipoglycopeptides with antimicrobial activities against Gram-positive bacteria and have been reported to have Ca<sup>2+</sup> dependency. Based on the biosynthetic gene cluster composition, the heterocylic polypeptide produced from the <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 is identical to that reported for gausemycin A produced by <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 (βAla1-Orn2-Ahpb3-hGlu4-Tyr5-Dab6-D-Leu7-Asp8-Gly9-Ser10-Gly11-ClKyn12-Ala13-Pro14) [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>] (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f007"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/9c3bb34e00a8/marinedrugs-23-00362-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="376" width="734" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Structure of the biosynthetic gene cluster, from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101, presumed to produce gausemycins. The substrates of the NRPS modules are indicated. * Two ORFS (ctg_618 and ctg_619) are homologous to a single NRPS module from the gausemycin BGC from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>]. These ORFs are predicted to incorporate <span class="font-variant-small-caps">d</span>-Leucine into the polypeptide. ** An ORF not annotated by antiSMASH to be part of the <em>gauA</em> NRPS is homologous to a NRPS module in the <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812, within <em>gauA</em>, and incorporates β-alanine into the polypeptide.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Unlike the gausemycins, which comprise 9 amino acid residues in the heterocycle, and exocyclic 5 amino acids—including non-proteogenic components—cadasides comprise 9 amino acids in the heterocycle with 4 exocyclic residues, while friulimicins comprise 10 amino acids in the heterocycle with one exocyclic residue. While the BGC of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 hosts 56 ORFs (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S9</a>) spanning 111.8 Kb, the gausemycins cluster of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 comprises 68 ORFs and is 121.5 Kb in length. A comparison of the two clusters reveals that 35 ORFs are homologous with 34.6–99.1% amino acid identities (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S33 and Table S10</a>). While the <em>gauA</em> NRPS of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 (Accession no. <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/protein/QWT72279.1" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">QWT72279.1</a>) is annotated as comprising 8448 amino acids, incorporating 6 moieties into the polypeptide, that of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 (ORF 33: <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>; <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S9</a>) is annotated as a 5910 amino acid NRPS incorporating 4 amino acids into the peptide. However, ORFs 31, 32, and 34 are annotated as separate NRPS genes, and along with ORF 33, they are predicted to produce an identical polypeptide to that of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812. The homologues of <em>gauB</em>, <em>gauC</em>, and <em>gauD</em> in the genome of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 share amino acid sequence identities of 93.9%, 89.8%, and 92.4%, respectively (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Supplementary Table S10</a>).</p>
<p>Notwithstanding that the orientation of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 BGC and <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 is different, ORF 9 to ORF 50 in the former is largely homologous to ORF 11 to ORF 44 in the latter. The main differences are the insertions of genes encoding a pyridoxal-phosphate dependent enzyme; cysteine synthase (ORF 13), an argininosuccinate lyase/adenylosuccinate lyase (ORF 15), and a methyltransferase (ORF 26) in the BGC of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101, which are absent from the gene cluster of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812, while the latter cluster hosts a flavin reductase family protein encoding gene (ORF 33) and a cation/H(+) antiporter encoding gene (ORF 45) not present in the cluster of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101. A biosynthesis schema has been proposed by Tyurin and colleagues [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>], with a similar pathway likely to be present in <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101. In some cases, single BGCs are known to produce more than one metabolite [<a href="#B60-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-marinedrugs-23-00362">60</a>] and differences between the gausemycin BGC from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812 and the NRPS/betalactone cluster from <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 may result in the production of different minor metabolites. As highlighted in <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>, while a number of new gausemycin analogues have been identified in the metabolome of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 as visualised through the molecular network annotation, attempts to isolate these new analogues have to date been unsuccessful. Interestingly, ORF 51 in the BGC of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 encodes an IS3 family transposase, suggesting the potential for horizontal gene transfer of part or all of this cluster.</p></section><section id="sec2dot6-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. The Proposed Biosynthesis of Ageloline A-D (<em><strong>1</strong></em>–<em><strong>4</strong></em>)</h3>
<p>The agelolines A–D are natural derivatives of 7-chlorokynurenic acid, partially biosynthesized from the gausemycin biosynthetic gene cluster (BGC), as evidenced by their shared chlorinated moiety. Ageloline A is proposed to originate from the kynurenine pathway, beginning with halogenation of L-tryptophan by ORF9, followed by conversion to 7-chlorokynurenine via ORF10, annotated as tryptophan 2,3-dioxygenase (antiSMASH) and <em>kynA</em> (PROKKA version 1.14.5). A final transamination step, likely catalysed by a kynurenine aminotransferase (KAT), completes the biosynthesis (See <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S10</a> and <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>). ORF6, annotated as a histidinol-phosphate aminotransferase (<em>hisC</em>), may fulfil this role despite histidinols not being typical KAT substrates [<a href="#B61-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-marinedrugs-23-00362">61</a>].</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-f008"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=12471526_marinedrugs-23-00362-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/c491/12471526/cd3260335ada/marinedrugs-23-00362-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="447" width="764" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/marinedrugs-23-00362-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Proposed biosynthetic scheme for ageloline A–D (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>4</strong>).</p></figcaption></figure><p>Agelolines B and C follow similar initial steps, diverging through distinct tailoring reactions. Ageloline B requires acetylation of 7-chlorokynurenine, yet no acetyltransferase is encoded within or near the BGC, which suggests the likely involvement of a distal genomic element; the closest candidate lies 84.6 Kb upstream, annotated as an L-methionine sulfoximine/sulfone acetyltransferase. Ageloline C is methylated via a methyltransferase encoded by ORF2, which is absent in the gausemycin-producing <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. INA-Ac-5812, suggesting strain-specific biosynthetic capabilities. Ageloline D, a chlorinated anthranilic acid, is likely formed via kynureninase (<em>kynU</em>) activity on chlorinated L-kynurenine. ORF11, annotated as an α-/β-hydrolase (antiSMASH) and <em>kynU</em> (PROKKA), is presumed to catalyse this conversion (<a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S10</a> and <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>).</p>
<p>Together, these findings suggest that agelolines are derived from a common halogenated intermediate, with diversification driven by distal or strain-specific tailoring enzymes. Annotation discrepancies between antiSMASH and PROKKA highlight the need for functional validation, while the absence of key enzymes within the BGC points to broader genomic involvement in ageloline biosynthesis.</p></section><section id="sec2dot7-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Biological Evaluation of Compounds <em><strong>1</strong></em>–<em><strong>5</strong></em>
</h3>
<p>Compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong> were initially evaluated for their antibacterial activity against the aquaculture fish pathogen <em>A. salmonicida</em> using the enzymatic cell-based assay previously described. Only compound <strong>5</strong> exhibited considerable activity at an MIC value of 32 µg/mL (see <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S40A</a>). However, the screening platform was expanded to accommodate human pathogens, including <em>S. aureus</em> ATCC6538P, <em>S. aureus</em> GISA L3797, <em>S. pneumoniae</em> L44, <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125, <em>P. aeruginosa</em> PA01, and <em>E. coli</em> ΔtolC L4242. Compound <strong>3</strong> showed significant to moderate inhibition against <em>Staphylococcus aureus</em> ATCC6538P, <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125, and <em>S. pneumoniae</em> L44 with MIC values of 6, 32, and 64 µg/mL, respectively, while moderate inhibition against <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125 was observed for <strong>2</strong> and <strong>4</strong> (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>; <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figures S40 and S41</a>).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-t002"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 2.</h4>
<div class="caption p"><p>In-vitro antimicrobial activity of compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong>.</p></div>
<div class="tbl-box p" tabindex="0"><table class="content" frame="hsides" rules="groups">
<thead>
<tr><th colspan="6" align="center" valign="middle" style="border-top:solid thin;border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1">Compound MIC (µg/mL)</th></tr>
<tr>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">Pathogenic Species</th>
<th align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">1</th>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/marinedrugs-23-00362-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>A recent discovery reported that the bioactivity of compound <strong>5</strong> against methicillin-resistant <em>S. aureus</em> increases with the addition of calcium to the cell culture—an observation that led to a successful patent application [<a href="#B62-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-marinedrugs-23-00362">62</a>]. Our experiment confirmed this finding and extended it beyond Gram-positive <em>S. aureus</em> to Gram-negative <em>A. salmonicida</em>, showing an 8- and 2-fold increase in activity with MIC values of 4 µg/mL and 16 µg/mL, respectively, when the assay cell culture broths were supplemented with 0.45 mM of calcium chloride. In contrast, increasing the calcium concentration to 20 mM yielded no further enhancement of the activity of <strong>5</strong> against any of the tested pathogens (<a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Figure S42</a>).</p>
<section class="tw xbox font-sm" id="marinedrugs-23-00362-t003"><h4 class="obj_head">Table 3.</h4>
