<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/marinedrugs-23-00362-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>It is quite unusual for cyclic glycolipopeptides to display activity against Gram-negative bacteria, as most antibiotics of these structural scaffolds are prevented from crossing the extra outer membrane (OM) barrier consisting of lipopolysaccharides (LPS) [<a href="#B63-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-marinedrugs-23-00362">63</a>,<a href="#B64-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-marinedrugs-23-00362">64</a>].</p>
<p>The outer layer is negatively charged and creates a permeability barrier that repels most antibiotics partly due to their large size [<a href="#B63-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-marinedrugs-23-00362">63</a>,<a href="#B64-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-marinedrugs-23-00362">64</a>,<a href="#B65-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-marinedrugs-23-00362">65</a>]. It was found that gausemycins are species-specific, as a wide range of antimicrobial inhibitions were observed when tested against a panel of Gram-positive bacteria [<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>], indicating multiple molecular targets, including membrane disruption in a Ca<sup>2+</sup>-dependent manner and channel-forming activity at a higher concentration of calcium ions [<a href="#B49-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-marinedrugs-23-00362">49</a>,<a href="#B50-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-marinedrugs-23-00362">50</a>].</p>
<p>This study presents the first report of compound <strong>5</strong> exhibiting species-specific inhibition of <em>Aeromonas salmonicida</em> while other Gram-negative strains—including <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125, <em>P. aeruginosa</em> PA01, and <em>E. coli</em> ΔtolC L4242—remained unaffected, regardless of calcium salt supplementation. This selective activity may be attributed to the distinctive structural and antigenic properties of the surface layers (S-layer) of the <em>A. salmonicida</em> outer membrane, which differ substantially from those of other Gram-negative bacteria [<a href="#B66-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-marinedrugs-23-00362">66</a>,<a href="#B67-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-marinedrugs-23-00362">67</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec3-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. General Experimental Procedures</h3>
<p>UV spectra were recorded on a Thermo Scientific UPLC system coupled to a photodiode array detector (Thermo Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA). Optical rotations were measured on an ADP 410 digital polarimeter (Bellingham + Stanley Ltd., Nottingham, UK). NMR spectra were recorded on Bruker AVANCE III spectrometers at 600 MHz (Bruker Corporation, Billerica, MA, USA). The Thermo Instruments UPLC system was connected to the Orbitrap IQ-X MS (Thermo Scientific, San Jose, CA, USA), equipped with heated electrospray ionisation (H-ESI). The Orbitrap was used to analyse the biological replicate samples under the following operating conditions: capillary temperature 275 °C, auxiliary gas flow rate 10 arbitrary units, sheath gas flow rate 50 arbitrary units, static gas mode, sweet gas (Arb) 1.0, ion transfer tube temperature 275 °C, vaporiser temperature 350 °C, capillary positive ion spray voltage 3.5k, and a lock mass correction set at RunStart EASY-IC. The full MS was set with a scan mass range of 150–2000 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>, Orbitrap resolution at 120,000, RF Lens at 35%, absolute AGC target at 4.000 × 105, maximum injection time mode at Auto, and data type set to centroid with the data-dependent acquisition mode set at Cycle Time (Top Speed). The assisted HCD (high-energy collisional dissociation) energy was set at 30%, with an activation time of 10 milliseconds, and the Orbitrap resolution was set at 60,000 for the MS/MS fragmentation step.</p>
<p>The LC system consists of a diode array detector (DAD) with a variable wavelength detection range of 200–800 nm, and an analytical Kinetex 2.6 µm EVO C18 column (100 Å, 100 × 2.1 mm). A mobile phase gradient of 0.1% formic acid in LC-MS grade H<sub>2</sub>O: acetonitrile from 5 to 95% in 13 min, and isocratic elution at 95% for 5 min at a flow rate of 300 µL/min with MS acquisition for 17 min, was employed in the UPLC and MS system.</p></section><section id="sec3dot2-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Microbial Isolation and Identification</h3>
<p><em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was isolated from the deep-sea sponge <em>Lissodendoryx diversichela</em> as previously described [<a href="#B26-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-marinedrugs-23-00362">26</a>]. The genome of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was sequenced using PacBio Sequel II following library preparation using SMRTbell Express Template Prep Kit 2.0, by Novogene Co. Ltd., Cambridge, UK. The genome was assembled using SPAdes version 3.11.1 [<a href="#B68-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-marinedrugs-23-00362">68</a>]. The assembled genome was annotated using Prokka v1.14.5 [<a href="#B69-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-marinedrugs-23-00362">69</a>]. Taxonomic assignment and phylogenetic analysis were performed using the Genome Taxonomy Database (GTDB-Tk) v2.3.2 [<a href="#B70-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-marinedrugs-23-00362">70</a>], which was implemented on the KBase web platform [<a href="#B70-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-marinedrugs-23-00362">70</a>,<a href="#B71-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-marinedrugs-23-00362">71</a>]. Biosynthetic gene clusters were identified using antiSMASH v 7.0 [<a href="#B72-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-marinedrugs-23-00362">72</a>], cblaster [<a href="#B73-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-marinedrugs-23-00362">73</a>], and clinker [<a href="#B74-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-marinedrugs-23-00362">74</a>].</p></section><section id="sec3dot3-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Small Fermentation of Streptomyces sp. B188M101 Using an OSMAC Approach</h3>
<p>A seed culture of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was prepared by inoculating an autoclaved 50 mL ISP2 medium (malt extract 10 g, yeast extract 4 g, and glucose 4 g in 1 L of milli-Q H<sub>2</sub>O, pH adjusted to 7.2) in a 250 mL Erlenmeyer baffled flask with a pure colony of the bacterial strain from an agar plate. Incubation of the seed culture lasted for 72 h at 28 °C and 180 rpm in a closed-system rotary shaker (SciQuip, Rotherham, UK). Following an OSMAC approach, three biological replicates of 200 mL media culture consisting of SGG-(a-c) [glycerol 10 g, soluble starch 10 g, corn steep powder 2.5 g, yeast extract 2 g, peptone 5 g, CaCO<sub>3</sub> 3 g, NaCl 1 g, in 1 L H<sub>2</sub>O, pH adjusted to 7.3], M400-(a-c) [1% glucose, 0.03% meat extract, 0.3% peptone, 2% soluble starch, 0.5% yeast extract, 0.3% CaCO<sub>3</sub>), and R358-(a-c)[0.2% peptone, 0.4% yeast, 1% soluble starch, 4% sea salt, 5 mL/L of an 8 g/L FeSO<sub>4</sub>∙7H<sub>2</sub>O stock solution, 5 mL/L of an 8 g/L KBr stock solution, 4% sigma sea salts] in 1L Erlenmeyer baffled flasks were further inoculated with 2 mL of the seed culture and incubated for 10 days under the same shaking conditions as the seed culture. Alongside the bacterial cultures, the blank controls consisting of 200 mL of the three uncultured media were prepared to remove interfering metabolites from the culture media. After fermentation, an autoclaved Dianion HP-20 absorbent resin (12 g/200 mL) was added to each of the culture broths and allowed to shake for another 7 h. The HP-20 adsorbent resins were filtered under a vacuum, and each culture broth resin and the respective process blank medium was placed in a separate, cleaned 1 L Erlenmeyer flask containing 200 mL of 100% methanol to macerate for 24 h. Complete extraction of the biomass was achieved after subsequent cycles of extraction with MeOH (100 mL) and ethyl acetate (100 mL). The combined extract was concentrated under reduced pressure with a vacuum rotatory evaporator (IKA VACSTAR digital, Staufen im Breisgau, Germany) and later dried completely in a nitrogen drier yielding 225.70 mg (SGG-a), 255.20 mg (SGG-b), 247.20 mg (SGG-c), 250.00 mg (M400-a), 176.00 mg (M400-b), 207.80 mg (M400-c), 264.80 mg (R358-a), 297.50 mg (R358-b), 310.50 mg (R358-c), 89.05 mg (SGG-blank), 85.05 mg (M400-blank), and 71.12 mg (R358-blank).</p></section><section id="sec3dot4-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Disc Diffusion Assay of Crude Extracts</h3>
<p>The antibacterial activity of extracts M400, R358, and SGG was tested using a disc diffusion assay following a previously described method [<a href="#B40-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-marinedrugs-23-00362">40</a>,<a href="#B55-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-marinedrugs-23-00362">55</a>]. Therefore, 8 mg/mL concentration solutions of the extracts and extracts’ media controls were prepared, loaded on sterile filter paper (6 mm disc), and allowed to dry. The inoculum pathogen broth (2 mL) prepared according to the 0.5 McFarland standard was spread on Mueller Hinton agar plates. Sterile dried filter paper discs loaded with methanol were used as the negative control and a 10 µg concentration of gentamicin discs (ThermoFisher Scientific Ltd, Cheshire, UK) as the positive control. The extract filter paper, media blanks, solvent control, and antibiotic discs were impregnated into the agar plates and incubated at 28 °C for 24 h and the diameter of inhibition zones was measured. Antibacterial activity was evaluated by measuring the zone of inhibition around the disc using a transparent millimetre scale. The bioassay experiment was repeated twice.</p></section><section id="sec3dot5-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Large-Scale Fermentation of Streptomyces sp. B188M101</h3>
<p>The large-scale fermentation (8 L) was prepared by the following process: 5 mL of a 200 mL seed culture of <em>Streptomyces</em> sp. B188M101 was transferred with a disposable sterile serological pipet into 40 × 1 L baffled Erlenmeyer flasks containing autoclaved 200 mL R358 media solution. After inoculation, they were incubated for 10 days at 28 °C and shaken at 180 rpm in a closed system shaker. When fermentation was completed, 12 g of autoclaved Diaion HP-20 resin was added to each flask and allowed to shake for 24 h before removing the resin and subsequently extracted with methanol (800 mL × 3) followed by ethyl acetate (300 mL × 2). The combined organic extract was dried and yielded 16.45 g of crude extract.</p></section><section id="sec3dot6-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Fractionation and Isolation of Metabolites from Streptomyces sp. B188M101</h3>
<p>The R358 crude extract (16.45 g) was subjected to fractionation by Kupchan partitioning [<a href="#B55-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-marinedrugs-23-00362">55</a>]. The extract was partitioned between aqueous (H<sub>2</sub>O, 700 mL) and organic phases (dichloromethane, 700 mL) in a 2 L round bottom flask. The aqueous phase (16.15 g) was further extracted with <em>sec</em>-butanol (300 mL × 3) yielding FB (3.85 g) and FW (8.80 g). The dichloromethane fraction (210 mg) was discarded upon chemical analysis. The FB fraction was chromatographed using a reveries BUCHI preparative flash system (Flawil, Switzerland) equipped with a reversed-phase EcoFlex C18 cartridge (80 g, 50 µm), stationary phase cartridge, and variable UV and ELSD detectors to monitor the run at 210, 230, and 254 nm. Chromatographic elution started with a mobile isocratic scheme of 100% H<sub>2</sub>O and run for 7 min followed by linear gradient elution to 100% MeOH for 80 min and finally maintained at 100% MeOH for 20 min at the flow rate of 7 mL /min with a combined running time of 94 min to afford FB1 (225.08 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 2–13 min), FB2 (111.28 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 15–19 min), FB3(97.55 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 20–26 min), FB4 (76.17 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 29–39 min), FB5 (32.38 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 45–48 min), FB6 (20.29 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 49–53 min), FB7 (31.62 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 56–62 min), FB8 (35.66 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 63–66 min), FB9(20.13 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 67–70 min), FB10 (17.59 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 71–75 min), FB11 (27.52 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 76–80 min), and FB12 (61.52 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 83–89 min). Following the chemical dereplication of the FB fractions, the FB6-FB9 fractions (107.7 mg) were combined and purified using a reversed-phase HPLC, ACE (5 µm 10 × 250 mm) with a gradient elution of 95: 5% H<sub>2</sub>O: MeCN for 15 min to 38% MeCN, followed by a gradient elution for 30 min to 50% H<sub>2</sub>O: MeCN and continued for another 20 min to 80% MeCN at the flow rate of 2 mL/min for a combined run of 50 min yielding co-eluting gausemycin derivatives (6.12 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 20–23 min) and compounds <strong>1</strong> (3.5 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 26.2 min), <strong>2</strong> (0.8 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 34.8 min), <strong>3</strong> (5 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 30.4 min), and <strong>4</strong> (5 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 27.1 min). The gausemycin mixture was further purified by re-injection with a starting gradient elution of 90:5% H<sub>2</sub>O: MeCN to 20% MeCN at 40 min, 38% MeCN at 70 min up to 50% MeCN for 80 min at the flow rate of 2 mL/min, yielding gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>) (2.7 mg, t<sub>R</sub> 55 min).</p>
<p>Ageloline B (<strong>1</strong>): yellow powder; [α]<sub>D</sub><sup>25</sup> −17.7 (c 0.225, MeOH); UV (MeOH) λ<sub>max</sub>, nm (log ε): 194 (2.94); 234(2.91), 266(2.64), 358(3.41); HRESIMS (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>): [M+H]<sup>+</sup> calcd. for C<sub>12</sub>H<sub>14</sub>ClN<sub>2</sub>O<sub>4</sub>, 285.0638; found 285.0607(Δ: +0.5 ppm); NMR data, see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S2 and Figures S11–S17 and S43</a>.</p>
<p>Ageloline C (<strong>2</strong>): light yellowish powder; HRESIMS (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>): [α]<sub>D</sub><sup>25</sup> −40 (c = 0.01 g/mL, MeOH); UV (MeCN-H<sub>2</sub>O)) λ<sub>max</sub>, nm (log ε): 194 (3.46); 234 (3.63), 254(3.24), 362(3.03); [M+H]<sup>+</sup> calcd. for C<sub>13</sub>H<sub>16</sub>ClO<sub>4</sub>N<sub>2</sub>, 299.0795; found, 299.0793 (Δ: +0.5 ppm); NMR data, see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S3 and Figures S18–S22 and S44</a>.</p>
<p>Ageloline D (<strong>3</strong>): yellow powder; HRESIMS (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em>); UV (MeCN-H<sub>2</sub>O λ<sub>max</sub>, nm (log ε): 194 (2.86); 222 (2.33), 254(2.89), 346(2.66); [M+H]<sup>+</sup> calcd. for C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>7</sub>ClO<sub>4</sub>N<sub>2</sub>, 172.0163; found, 172.0160 (Δ: +1.3 ppm); NMR data, see <a href="#marinedrugs-23-00362-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a> and <a href="#app1-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link">Table S4 and Figures S24–S28 and S45</a>.</p></section><section id="sec3dot7-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7. Antimicrobial Activity</h3>
<p>The antibacterial activity of compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong> was determined against strains from the NAICONS collection of bacterial pathogens, including ATCC strains, as follows: 90 μL of 1–6 × 10<sup>5</sup> CFU/mL bacterial suspension was dispensed into each well of a 96-well plate containing 10 μL of serial 1:2 dilutions of compound <strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong> (from a 10 mg/mL stock solution in 100% DMSO). All the strains were grown in Müller Hinton broth except for <em>Streptococcus</em> spp. and <em>Candida albicans</em> strains, which were grown in Todd Hewitt broth and Yeast Peptone Dextrose medium, respectively. Sterile solutions of CaCl<sub>2</sub> in water were added to the media at the time of the experiments to obtain the final concentrations of 450 µM or 20 mM. Plates were incubated at 37 °C in a Synergy2 microplate reader (BioTek Instruments, Winooski, VT, USA) with readings at 595 nm registered every hour.</p></section><section id="sec3dot8-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.8. MZmine 4.20 Data Pre-Processing, MetaboAnalyst 5.0, and Feature-Based Molecular Networking</h3>
<p>The biological replicate MS data was converted into mzML format using MSConvert from the ProteoWizard [<a href="#B42-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-marinedrugs-23-00362">42</a>] and imported to MZmine (version 4.2.0), a scalable MS data analysis platform that supports hybrid datasets from various instrumental setups [<a href="#B43-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-marinedrugs-23-00362">43</a>]. The mzML data was pre-processed with mass ion detection achieved through the removal of MS1 and MS2 baseline noise levels set to 1.0 × 10<sup>4</sup> and 1.0 × 10<sup>2</sup>, respectively. The chromatogram construction was performed for each of the centroided MS1 detected using the ADAP chromatogram builder, where minimum consecutive scans, minimum intensity for consecutive scans, minimum absolute height, and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> mass tolerance were set to 4, 1.0 × 10<sup>2</sup>, 1.0 × 10<sup>4</sup>, and 5 ppm, respectively. The peak detection utilised local minimum resolver as a deconvolution algorithm to construct the chromatogram of each mass ion while keeping the original feature list created in the previous step. For this algorithm, the chromatographic threshold was set to 85%, the minimum search range in the RT absolute range to 0.05 min, the minimum relative height to 0.0%, the minimum absolute height to 2.0 × 10<sup>6</sup>, and the minimum ratio of peak top/edge to 1.70. The identified peaks were deisotoped using the isotopic peaks grouper, in which the <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> tolerance (intra-sample) was set to 5 ppm, the RT tolerance to 0.02 for absolute minutes, maximum charge of 3 absolute minutes, and the representative isotope as the most intense. The deisotoped peaks were then aligned using a join aligner to correct any deviation of the retention time of each mass peak: the ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> tolerance for the alignment was set to 10 ppm, retention time tolerance to 0.06 at absolute minute, weight for <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> and RT set to 75 and 25, respectively, and the mobility weight was set to 1. The peak list produced after the alignment step was filtered using a feature list row filter algorithm to remove any mass ions outside the set range of 100 to 2000 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> and keep peak rows that match all criteria.</p>
<p>The resulting feature list, including the retention time, mass to charge ratio, and peak area rows, was exported by converting into a MetaboAnalyst format CSV file, a text format that presents a set of data in Excel style. For this study, CSV files were generated in triplicate for each biological replicate sample and converted to a zip folder before uploading to the MetaboAnalyst 5.0 platform with the option of One Factor-Statistical Analysis [<a href="#B75-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-marinedrugs-23-00362">75</a>]. The processed MS peak list data was pre-processed by setting the mass tolerance and retention time tolerance to 0.025 and 30 s, respectively. All features with missing values generated due to poor repeatability and below the limit of detection in the triplicates were replaced with 1/5 of the minimum positive values below their corresponding values by setting the threshold to 20%. The features were further filtered using the interquartile range (IQR), with the relative standard deviations (RSDs) set at 25%. Normalization by sum was performed for the feature peak list, followed by log transformation (base 10) and pareto scaling to transform the data to more a Gaussian-type normal distribution that allows for easy comparability in a graphical style. The above-mentioned parameters were used to generate the Principal Component Analysis (PCA), Dendrogram, and Heatmaps used in this study.</p>
<p>The respective MS feature lists of MS1 and MS2 were exported as quant.csv and MGF files—the formats used to create Feature-Based Molecular Networking (FBMN) analysis on the Global Natural Social Networking (GNPS) [<a href="#B41-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-marinedrugs-23-00362">41</a>,<a href="#B76-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-marinedrugs-23-00362">76</a>]. For generating the networking, both the precursor ion and MS/MS fragment ion tolerance were set to 0.05 Da. A molecular network was then created with edges filtered to have a cosine score above 0.50 and at least six matched peaks. The top 20 most similar nodes were kept. The spectra in the network were then searched against GNPS spectral libraries [<a href="#B76-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-marinedrugs-23-00362">76</a>]. The DEREPLICATOR was used to annotate MS/MS spectra [<a href="#B77-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-marinedrugs-23-00362">77</a>] and the molecular networks generated were visualized using Cytoscape software version 3.10.0 [<a href="#B37-marinedrugs-23-00362" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-marinedrugs-23-00362">37</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec4-marinedrugs-23-00362"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">4. Conclusions</h2>
<p>In this study, we isolated and structurally elucidated three new chlorinated natural products, agelolines B–D (<strong>1</strong>–<strong>3</strong>), alongside two previously reported metabolites, ageloline A (<strong>4</strong>) and gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>), using metabolomic analysis combined with bioassay-guided purification. The antimicrobial activity of compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>5</strong> was assessed against the fish pathogen <em>A. salmonicida</em>, with gausemycin A (<strong>5</strong>) exhibiting pronounced activity at the minimum inhibitory concentration (MIC). Subsequent screening against a panel of human pathogens revealed that compound <strong>3</strong> displayed notable inhibitory effects against <em>S. aureus</em> ATCC6538P and <em>S. maltophilia</em> L2125. Interestingly, the addition of calcium salts enhanced the activity of compound <strong>5</strong> against <em>A. salmonicida</em>, resulting in a two-fold increase in potency.</p>
<p>Elucidating the mechanistic basis underlying the selective inhibition of <em>A. salmonicida</em> by compound <strong>5</strong> remains a central focus of our future investigations. To date, lipoglycopeptide antibiotics have not been reported to exhibit such activity against Gram-negative bacteria, highlighting the novelty of our findings. To support further biological and structural studies, efforts are currently underway to upscale the isolation and characterization of the newly identified gausemycin congeners dereplicated in this study.</p></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
<p>We would like to thank Russell Gray of the Marine Biodiscovery Centre, Aberdeen for assisting in NMR spectroscopy; Diana Morgun for providing support and assistance in the disc diffusion assay screening; and Haniel Oluwabusola for drawing the graphics using Medi Bang Paint (MediBang Inc., Tokyo, Japan).
